Neanderthal heel bones reveal feet built for a demanding life

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Neanderthals may have had flatter feet that helped them handle heavy loads and stay steady on rough ground.

A flatter foot may have helped Neanderthals support their heavy bodies and stay steady on rough ground.

A new study of fossil heel bones suggests that Neanderthal feet usually rolled inward and had a lower arch along the outside edge.

Researchers think this build may have suited the demands of their daily lives.

The study also found differences within our own species, Homo sapiens.

People who lived in Bologna, Italy, in the 19th and 20th centuries had slimmer heel bones than prehistoric hunter-gatherers, and their footwear and less active lives may help explain why.

Rita Sorrentino, now a professor at the University of Palermo, led the study, which University of Bologna professor Maria Giovanna Belcastro coordinated.

Belcastro said the analysis allowed the team “to highlight both an extraordinary biomechanical adaptation to withstand heavy loads and rough terrains, and the impact of modern life and footwear on our feet.”

No one can watch a Neanderthal walk, so the researchers looked for clues in the bones left behind. They used laser scans and CT scans to create 3D models of 71 adult heel bones.

Seven belonged to Neanderthals from sites in France, Spain, and Belgium. The youngest came from Spy Cave in Belgium and belonged to one of the last known Neanderthals in Europe.

The other 64 belonged to Homo sapiens. The oldest came from hunter-gatherers at Qafzeh and Skhul caves in Israel and dated to between 120,000 and 90,000 years ago.

The sample also included later Ice Age hunter-gatherers, Iron Age farmers from Sicily, and 46 people from a Bologna cemetery collection.

The researchers considered the Bologna group the least physically active population in the study.

The researchers marked 276 points along the edges and curved surfaces of each digital bone.

These points let them compare the heels in detail, while software estimated missing points on broken fossils from the parts that remained.

Neanderthal heels stood out in every comparison with Homo sapiens.

They were shorter from front to back, wider from side to side, and taller, although their overall size fell within the range seen in our species.

The biggest difference appeared at the back of the heel, where the Achilles tendon attaches. From behind, this area looked oval in Neanderthals and more triangular in Homo sapiens.

Neanderthals also had a much smaller bump underneath the heel, where a ligament that supports the arch attaches.

The researchers suggest this ligament may have provided less support, allowing the foot to bend more easily.

Belcastro linked the sturdy heels to the rest of the Neanderthal body.

“The robustness of the heel reflects the overall body structure of Neanderthals, which is characterized by a remarkable muscular and bone mass and an extremely intense level of physical activity,” Belcastro said.

“These physical characteristics indicate an adaptation aimed at promoting a greater load-bearing capacity on the back of the foot and a high mobility on uneven ground.”

The joints offered another clue. Where the heel meets the ankle bone, the joint surfaces were flatter and wider toward the outside of the foot.

That shape suggests Neanderthal feet normally rolled inward, a movement specialists call pronation.

Earlier research by Sorrentino’s group found signs of the same posture in Neanderthal ankle bones, on the other side of this joint.

In living people, rolling the foot inward brings more of those joint surfaces into contact and helps spread the load.

Neanderthals are thought to have carried more body mass than Homo sapiens, including more muscle, so this posture may have helped support their weight.

The researchers also propose that Neanderthals had a lower outer arch and a more flexible middle section of the foot.

Together, those features may have helped them stay steady as they crossed uneven ground.

An earlier study by the team found that a Neanderthal midfoot bone shared some features with those of adults diagnosed with flexible flatfoot. Most of those adults were overweight or obese.

Extra body weight may make feet roll inward and flatten their arches in people today. Still, similarities in bone shape do not prove that Neanderthal feet worked in exactly the same way.

Some differences may reflect ancestry rather than daily habits.

A small tunnel between the heel and ankle bones was shortest in Neanderthals, and its short length in other ancient human fossils suggests an inherited trait.

Within Homo sapiens, however, heel shape tended to vary with lifestyle.

In some ways, hunter-gatherers and even Iron Age farmers who also foraged had heels more like those of Neanderthals than those of the Bologna group.

Fossil footprints suggest that Neanderthals in Europe and later Ice Age Homo sapiens walked barefoot.

The Bologna group habitually wore heavy shoes and boots at work, and their heels differed from those of every earlier Homo sapiens group in the study.

Rigid footwear limits how far a foot can tilt from side to side.

The researchers suggest this restriction helped shape the joint at the front of the Bologna heels, which was taller and more curved.

The team also linked the Bologna group’s slimmer heels to footwear and a less active city life. But shoes alone cannot explain every difference.

“The morphology of our skeleton is not only the product of a strict genetic program, but also the consequence of physical stimuli and our daily life habits,” Belcastro said.

Ancestry, nutrition, and environment can also affect heel shape, the researchers wrote. The comparisons suggest that daily life matters, but they do not show how much each influence contributed.

The Neanderthal findings rest on just seven heels, several of which were damaged at the back and needed partial reconstruction.

The researchers also grouped men and women together because the samples did not allow them to test whether sex affected the results.

All the bones came from adults, leaving another question unanswered: how did Neanderthal heels develop that shape?

Their feet may have grown differently, responded to the strain of walking during childhood, or both.

A lower Neanderthal arch remains a hypothesis because bones alone cannot show exactly how a foot moved.

The researchers want future work to combine these shape comparisons with 3D models of foot motion to test the idea.

The study is published in the Journal of Human Evolution.

I Went Dumpster Diving At Wendy’s And Bought $100,000 In Stickers—All For Airline Miles

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

I found her video via Mommy Points and genuinely laughed pretty hard, probably because it hits a bit close to home.

Every ridiculous instruction comes with absolute confidence. Don’t have a pet? Doesn’t matter. Buying all that dog food sounds expensive? It’s going to pay for itself. You’re confused? Just keep following the steps. We’ve got circles and arrows for you.

Watch Britt Migs explain “how those credit card point people sound.”

One commenter explains, “This doesn’t work if you already had a SwizzyPay account.” Someone else offers a real service to help people book lie flat seats, prompting Britt to reply, “omg i’ve summoned one of.”

That’s us. Someone says the process sounds exhausting and our instinct is to explain one more exception, certain that this will clear everything up.

The closest real example might be my strategy for the 2011 US Airways Grand Slam promotion. You earned a big bonus by completing transactions with different partners. Getting to 36 qualifying activities earned 100,000 bonus miles.

Transferred points from five different hotel programs. Converted four American Airlines miles into one US Airways mile. Spent 500 miles on a magazine subscription I didn’t want. Installed the airline’s browser toolbar, performed three searches to earn one mile, and then disabled the toolbar.

This isn’t even something from a less mature period of my life. Less than two weeks ago, I explained how I redirected my wife’s Bilt authorized-user rewards after using up my five points accelerators. Her points go to her account. Her separate Bilt Cash goes to her account, too. That lets her use her own accelerators and separate transfer bonus allowance. Then each to have a primary card and an authorized-user card on the other’s account to take advantage of double transfer bonuses.

The old deals had names like BigCrumbs, OneVanilla, and Bluebird. SwizzyPay (formerly Buck Buck) fits right in. Did you know that GooglePay used to be known as ISIS until the Islamic State ruined that brand? BigCrumbs, OneVanilla, and Bluebird. In 2014, I explained a chain involving buying American Express gift cards through a cash back portal, using those to buy OneVanilla Visa cards at CVS, loading the Visa balances onto Bluebird at Walmart, and moving the money back to a bank account. You needed stores that would cooperate. The cash back helped cover the fees. Walmart was too far away for the economics to work for me but I had alternatives! Christmas morning, buying six figures in stickers. In 2009, combining a US Airways shopping bonus with Track-It-Back’s earning rate produced 140 miles per dollar. But first you needed purchases from 5 total merchants to qualify (but not more than 10!). I arranged over $100,000 in sticker purchases across accounts belonging to friends and colleagues, generating over 16 million miles. This involved little purchases like $2 tree donations and $5 gift cards before the big orders. The product helped recover lost belongings. A charitable donation reduced the tax basis. I had no other use for the product. Handwrite 94 cards and send them to Kalamazoo. IHG’s 2015 promotion offered at least 500 points per mailed sweepstakes entry. Ninety-four separate entries meant at least 47,000 points. A reader offered to do all the handwriting and mailing for $136. I used the service. We had created an industry to spare people the manual labor of telling a hotel company their address 94 times. Buy money, deposit money, use the money to pay for the money. The appeal is that reading the rules can actually change the outcome. Most customers accept the price. We want to find the exception, prove it works, and tell someone about it.

It’s also a remarkable use of human effort. Shipping coins around the country and handwriting the same information 94 times can be profitable. And the person congratulating himself on outsmarting marketing may spend more time thinking about a bank’s products than the bank’s own employees.

The financial mistake is counting every benefit at full retail while forgetting the costs. A $12,000 business class ticket doesn’t mean you saved $12,000 if you’d never have bought it. Annual fees, purchases you wouldn’t otherwise make, forgone cash back, and an inconvenient itinerary are costs.

But the opposite mistake is pretending every spare hour could have been sold at your professional billing rate. Some people enjoy crossword puzzles or football. Some enjoy figuring out which airline program will book another airline’s seat for fewer points.

Dumpster diving? Oof, I see Gary’s taking things well… (c’mon, it’s Citi Nights! @L737…)

I spent much of 2015 trying to find an Office Max that still had a stash of $500 VISA gift cards so I could buy $1000s worth with a $15 discount per 2 $500 cards to meet minimum spends for SUBs. If there wasn’t an Office Max promo, I would drive 30 miles to the nearest Simon Mall to do the same because the activation fees were $4/ea instead of $5.95/ea. I would then activate the gift cards and go to Target to load them on to my RedBird debit card (remember K8?) and go home and pay off the original VISA bill to get my SUB.

Everyone was doing this back then as evidenced by the rack of $500 VISA gift cards being cleaned out at many of the Office Max stores. Good times.

The Canadian mint ran a similar deal but the coins were made of silver. I still have mine. Anyone else?!

Oh man, wish these were more commonplace these days. Sounds like fun!

@1990 — Who needs dumpsters when you can get 6x every Friday and Saturday night!?

Nvidia’s CEO is reportedly the target of a ‘Chinese spy plot’ for intelligence on American AI chips.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

China’s intelligence agency recruited Megaspeed executive Alice Huang to spy on Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, according to The New York Times. The Ministry of State Security (MSS) enlisted her to glean information about American AI chips and “potentially influence the company to push for its most advanced chips to be made available to China.” Megaspeed is a customer of Nvidia’s, and Alice Huang also has “relationships with executives at the highest levels of the A.I. chip industry,” including leaders at Gigabyte, the NYT says.

Last week, the NYT reported that Jensen Huang was seen as the target of a “Chinese spy plot” and shared details about Alice Huang and Jensen Huang’s relationship, but the information that China’s MSS had recruited her is new.

The NYT describes the relationship between Alice Huang and Jensen Huang, who are not related, as “close.” President Trump, who is friendly with Jensen Huang, was briefed last year about the relationship, but didn’t take any action. Yesterday, three House committees asked Jensen Huang to appear for a joint, closed-door briefing on his “interactions” with Alice Huang.

Three Games You Can Play For Free This Weekend On PC

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

It’s Friday! Welcome to the weekend. We made it. Halloween. Election day. The launch of The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake. We survived. I’m about to go pick up Chinese takeout for my family, but before I do, here are some good deals on PC games to kick things off in November.

First, we have games that are temporarily free on Steam. Payday 3, which remains in recovery mode after its terrible launch, is yours to try risk-free until November 9. You can go download it, grab some friends, and try to pull off a few heists without spending a dime. “Launched badly, but it’s slowly taking a turn for the better,” one Steam reviewer recently posted. “To me personally, I love it now, but I can also see that it needs some more spice to give it enough kick for everyone. Will keep supporting and keep genuinely hoping that it could pull a Cyberpunk 2077 moment.”

Second, we have Age of Wonders 4, a generally quite good 4X strategy RPG. It’s also available to play for free until November 9. If you want a fantasy game that mixes empire building with fun tactical battles, Age of Wonders 4strikes a pretty good balance. Players are still encountering bugs and multiplayer issues, but if you just want to settle in for some cozy city building and battles and have a PC with an Nvidia GeForce GTX 670 card or better, it’s a good deal.

Moving over to the Epic Games Store, floating city management sim Flotsam is free to claim until November 12. It’s a feel-good post-apocalyptic game about salvaging debris, meeting fellow survivors, and combing through skill trees to help rebuild civilization. It’s vibrant, chill, and normally $25. And unlike Age of Wonders 4 and Payday 3, it’ll be yours to keep. If you haven’t already played it, this one’s worth having in your library.

Finally, a bonus round. I don’t usually recommend Humble Bundle’s monthly subscription, but the games for November are wroth a look. If you’re willing to sign-up, members this month are getting PC keys for Mafia: The Old Country, The Outer Worlds 2, Shinobi: Art of Vengeance, and a bunch more games. Those three alone are well worth it, but horror shooter Crisol: Theater of Idols, which only released back in February, is also part of the mix. You can read our review of it here.

Transfer news LIVE: Arsenal eye triple deal; huge Man Utd manager twist; Chelsea, Liverpool, Tottenham latest

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The January window is fast approaching as Arsenal, Chelsea, Manchester United, Liverpool and Tottenham all pursue some huge new mid-season signings to help reach their goals. Arsenal are said to be targeting a move for Antonio Nusa as they also eye Eli Junior Kroupi and Kenan Yildiz, with the likes of Alex Baena also believed to be in Mikel Arteta’s sights along with Julian Alvarez.

Didier Deschamps has reportedly turned down the chance to manage Manchester United, where Sunday’s game against Aston Villa will be crucial to determining the future of under-fire boss Michael Carrick. Tottenham are understood to still retain matching rights for Harry Kane after the England captain signed a record-breaking new Bayern Munich contract that is believed to make him the highest-paid player in Europe in terms of base salary.

Chelsea are confident of signing Alex Scott when the market reopens in the New Year, while Liverpool are keen on a swoop for Mexico wonderkid Gilberto Mora and Lamine Camara as Trent Alexander-Arnold continues to be linked with a sensational switch back to Anfield. Follow all the latest transfer news, rumours and gossip live below!

Christos Tzolis has hailed the role played by Cristhian Mosquera to help him acclimatise to life at Arsenal.

The Greek international signed for the Gunners back in the summer to replace Leandro Trossard and Gabriel Martinelli on the left flank.

He has hit the ground running since his £34million move from Club Brugge,as he returned to the English top flight for the first time since 2024, when he ended a fruitless three-year spell at Norwich City.

Now, after some impressive performances across all competitions, the winger has revealed the Arsenal star that has helped to settle him in London.

Manchester United have been bolstered in their pursuit of a left-back.

The Red Devils have been hamstrung in that position this term, with natural options Luke Shaw and Patrick Dorgu both struck down with injuries that have kept them out for long spells.

As such, it has forced under-fire manager Michael Carrick to experiment with makeshift solutions in the interim, with right-backs Diogo Dalot and Noussair Mazraoui both depuitising there, while central defender Ayden Heaven has also been shifted out wide to cover with limited success.

Didier Deschamps has reportedly turned down an approach over the Manchester United manager’s job, as more details emerge over the future of Michael Carrick.

Carrick’s position at Old Trafford has been subject to severe scrutiny for several weeks now following a miserable eight-game winless run across all competitions that was the club’s worst since the 1988/89 season.

Such a dire sequence was only snapped on Tuesday night as Man Utd staged an incredible turnaround following an abysmal first half to beat depleted Roma 3-2 at home in the Champions League, though it remains to be seen how much respite that offered their beleaguered head coach.

Sky Sports report that there remains support for Carrick within the United hierarchy, who have been said to be split on a potential replacement amid strong links to the likes of former Newcastle boss Eddie Howe and ex-Germany manager Julian Nagelsmann.

Real Madrid look to have won the race for Manchester United sensation JJ Gabriel.

The teenage star shocked Old Trafford chiefs back in September by stating his desire to leave the club immediately, just days before he was expected to make his senior debut aged just 15.

All of the rest of the heavyweight clubs across Europe were inevitably interested in signing one of the brightest youth talents in England, but Real Madrid and Barcelona quickly emerged as the front runners.

Madrid were said to be leading the race, with Fabrizio Romano now reporting that Gabriel has chosen the Bernabeu and that his camp have informed all parties of his decision.

The move is expected to be sealed soon, with Man Utd likely only due minimal training compensation.

Tottenham retain matching rights on Harry Kane despite his new Bayern Munich contract, reports claim.

The England captain yesterday put pen to paper on a lucrative contract extension in Bavaria until 2029 that is said to make him the highest-paid player in Europe in terms of base salary.

That bumper new deal does not impact Tottenham’s first-option right to match any future bids for Kane, per The Telegraph.

That agreement was reached during his initial exit from Spurs in 2023 and is said to lie outside of his contract, so has been unaffected.

Didier Deschamps has reportedly told Manchester United that he is not interested in taking over as manager at Old Trafford.

Michael Carrick’s future as Man Utd boss remains hugely uncertain ahead of Sunday’s crunch home Premier League clash with Aston Villa, which is likely to prove pivotal.

Eddie Howe and Julian Nagelsmann had previously been considered as the leading candidates for the role if United - who will not consider an interim solution this time - were to sack Carrick, though FootMercato in France reported on Thursday that the club had also approached former long-time Les Bleus head coach Deschamps.

However, he is said to have told them that he currently has no interest in the job in another blow to the beleaguered Red Devils.

Arsenal are tracking Antonio Nusa ahead of the January transfer window, reports claim.

The Gunners are known to be keen on attacking reinforcements when the market reopens for business in the New Year and have reportedly had their eyes on Bournemouth forward Eli Junior Kroupi and Juventus winger Kenan Yildiz.

10 flu shot myths and what the CDC said about them

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

A 2011 gallery paired 12 flu vaccine fears with CDC answers. Here are 10 of those myths and what the agency said.

Flu season hands millions of Americans a small, personal choice. The decision takes a moment at a clinic, yet it rests as much on what people believe about the vaccine as on anything a doctor says. A person who trusts the shot books an appointment. A person who doubts it postpones the visit or skips it. Multiply those private calculations across the country, and beliefs about a single injection shape how families face the weeks ahead. The outcome touches more than the person in the chair. Coworkers, classmates, and relatives breathe the same air through those weeks.

Three worries keep surfacing as people weigh the shot. Some people fear the vaccine could trigger autism. Others suspect the shot delivers the illness it promises to prevent. A third group questions whether the injection accomplishes much of anything. Each fear carries a price. A parent worried about autism may keep a child unvaccinated. Someone convinced the needle brings on sickness may go without protection. A skeptic who sees no benefit has no reason to make the trip. Hesitation can also push the question down a crowded to-do list, where it can sit untouched. Answering these fears means setting each claim beside what public health authorities said about it, one at a time.

CBS News took on these objections in a photo gallery posted Dec. 6, 2011, as influenza made its yearly return. The gallery placed 12 widely held notions about influenza vaccines next to the position of the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. Each entry pairs a short statement of the myth with a brief rebuttal. Its corrections cite research in general terms and name no researchers, trials, or sample sizes. The evidence here reflects the agency's stance as the outlet relayed it. This article examines 10 of the gallery's 12 myths. Every answer that follows describes what the agency said in 2011, and readers should check current guidance before acting on any of it.

Flu shots contained only inactivated viruses in the 2011 season, and the CDC held that particles in that condition could not infect anyone. People who expect the needle to deliver the disease it targets misjudge what goes into the syringe. A dose of disabled virus has no way to start a case of influenza.

Salt-water placebo shots exposed only two complaints unique to the flu vaccine, redness where the needle entered and a sore arm. Researchers gave one group the vaccine and another an injection of salt water, then compared what each set of people reported. Both differences stayed confined to the injection site, and neither resembles a bout of flu. By this measure, the shot's distinct toll amounted to a tender, reddened patch of skin.

The CDC reports that needle-delivered flu vaccines rely on either inactivated viruses that cannot infect or proteins drawn from an influenza virus. The agency states that a flu vaccine cannot cause flu.

Flublok, a recombinant vaccine, comes from an insect cell line and uses no influenza viruses or eggs, according to the CDC. Flucelvax begins with influenza viruses grown in cell culture, which undergo inactivation and leave the finished product unable to give anyone influenza. The agency rules out influenza from either product. The two take separate paths to the same safety, one disabling the virus after growing it and the other never using it. Whatever the method of manufacture, none of these injected vaccines contains a virus able to cause disease.

Influenza that surfaces soon after a shot traces to a virus caught from another source. A person who catches the virus ahead of vaccination, or during the window before the body builds its protection, can fall ill. The CDC explains that the vaccine plays no part in such a case. Someone who gets sick after a flu shot has more reason to retrace recent exposures than to blame the vaccine.

A belief common in 2011 held that a flu shot lost its point once November ended, and the CDC's answer rejected that cutoff. Vaccination after that month kept paying off as long as influenza moved through communities. The myth turned a missed appointment into a reason to skip the whole season. Circulating virus set the real deadline.

Experts urged people to get immunized as soon as the vaccine became available that year. Early beat late, and late beat never. The advice named an early target without closing the door on later appointments. Anyone who missed the first weeks of supply had not lost the winter. The guidance treated a delayed shot as a fallback worth taking, and it gave no one grounds to pass on protection over a slow start.

Flu activity in the U.S. can begin climbing in October and hold on until May, according to the CDC. The CDC reports that activity tends to crest between December and February. Measured against that calendar, the heaviest stretch of illness arrives after the myth's supposed deadline has passed. Anyone who abandoned the shot under that logic would face the entire peak window unprotected.

The CDC repeated the point in its guidance for the 2022-2023 season, telling people who could not get vaccinated until November or later to go ahead at that point. The agency tied that advice to flu activity that can stay heavy into May. The agency's stance from 2011 held up in that later advice, and November offered no excuse to stay unvaccinated.

Early doses gave the fullest coverage, and later doses bought defense for the months when flu reached its height. A tardy start cost some weeks of protection without wiping out the payoff. By the myth's logic, the people most in need of a push, those already running behind, were the ones told to give up.

Last season's flu shot offered no promise of protection in 2011, according to the CDC position the gallery relayed. The gallery aimed that correction at people who believed a single shot keeps working for years. Flu viruses mutate from one year to the next, and each round of change leaves the previous formula a step behind. As a result, the vaccine got a fresh update every year. Anyone counting on a 2010 shot to carry them through the winter relied on a formula built for viruses that had since changed.

The slow pileup of small genetic mutations in influenza viruses goes by the name antigenic drift. The CDC's Pink Book identifies drift as the chief reason a person can catch influenza more than once. The same process explains why the makeup of flu vaccines faces review and revision each year. Each new recipe answers a virus that refuses to hold still.

Immunity from any one shot fades in less than a year for practical purposes, according to the Pink Book. Two forces drive the decline. The antibodies a vaccine prompts the body to make wane, and the circulating viruses keep drifting away from the strains the shot aimed at.

Strain match governs how much benefit a shot delivers. The payoff varies with the resemblance between vaccine strains and the strains spreading in a given season. Three other factors shape the result: the recipient's age, the recipient's health, and the type of vaccine. A shot designed around an earlier season's viruses starts behind on strain match. The strains it targets have had another year to evolve.

Yearly reformulation and the brief life of vaccine immunity turn the flu shot into a seasonal appointment. Each season resets the question, and the only shot that answers it is the one built for the viruses circulating that winter.

Government scientists in 2011 urged vaccinated people to keep up the everyday steps that stop germs and viruses, including those behind influenza, from moving between people. Under that guidance, a flu shot excused no one from ordinary hygiene. The myth treats vaccination as the last step anyone needs, and the 2011 position answered it by keeping those steps in place after the shot.

Tiny drops of fluid make the case for those routines. Experts believe the main way flu travels between people is through drops an infected person releases by coughing, sneezing, or speaking. Those drops can land in the mouth or nose of someone close by, who may breathe them into the lungs as well. A second, less common route runs through objects. Someone touches a surface holding flu virus and then touches the mouth or nose, and in some cases the eyes.

Feeling healthy offers no proof of being harmless to others. A person with flu may be able to pass the virus along before realizing they are sick, as well as during the illness. Silent spread gives daily safeguards a job on days a vaccinated person feels fine.

A few plain practices close off those paths. Covering the nose and mouth during a cough or sneeze might shield people nearby from illness. Keeping distance from others can lower the odds of handing a respiratory virus along. Regular handwashing protects against germs, and an alcohol-based hand rub fills in when soap and water are out of reach. Clean hands target the object route, where germs reach the eyes, nose, or mouth on a person's own fingers.

Government scientists asked parents to teach the same habits to their children, a request that extended the recommendation past the vaccinated adult and into the rest of the household. A shot works inside one body. Covered coughs, distance, and washed hands interrupt the trips flu takes between bodies, and the 2011 advice relied on both layers at once.

Holding off on a flu shot until people nearby fell sick opened a gap of about two weeks, the time the vaccine needed to deliver full protection under the CDC's position as of 2011. The myth cast vaccination as a response to someone else's illness. Its believers figured they needed a shot only once the flu had reached the people around them. Under that logic, a symptom-free social circle served as proof that the needle could wait.

Sick neighbors make a poor alarm. Waiting for others to fall ill was likely to mean acting too late, the CDC held. By the time a cough moves through an office or a household, the virus is circulating within arm's reach, and the person who held off has no head start. A visible case marks a moment when exposure may already have occurred. Symptoms in others tell a person about the past, and a vaccine plan built on them trails the virus.

Antibodies that guard against flu need that same stretch to form after vaccination, according to the CDC. A shot taken after a local wave of illness begins leaves its recipient unshielded during the window when the virus moves through family, friends, and coworkers. The body spends those days building its response while the virus keeps moving.

The myth also misreads what a flu shot does. Vaccination works by preparing the body before a threat appears. A flu shot behaves the way a smoke detector does, useful only if someone mounts it before the fire starts. Treating it as an emergency response turns a preventive tool into a gamble on timing.

The cost of delay lands on the person who waited. Each day spent watching others for signs of illness eats into the lead time the vaccine requires. Timing the vaccine to a local outbreak reverses the order that matters. Immunity has to be in place before exposure. The decision to get a shot belongs ahead of the first sniffle in anyone nearby.

Babies younger than six months can catch influenza, yet in 2011 the CDC considered them too young to receive a flu shot. The myth assumes anyone at risk can roll up a sleeve. Infants in their first half-year break that assumption. They face the virus with no vaccine of their own to stop it. Age alone kept the shot from them. Nothing in the CDC's answer suggested these babies resisted infection.

The CDC's 2011 remedy moved the defense one step outward. Its best safeguard for these infants was immunization for every other member of the household, along with the caregivers who tend the child. Parents, older siblings, and babysitters all fall inside that circle. A grandparent who watches the baby on weekends belongs in it as well. Each vaccinated person closes one route the virus could travel to reach the baby.

The guidance turns a personal health choice into a family one. An adult who skips the shot leaves a gap in the barrier around a child who cannot get one. With no shot to fall back on, the infant relies on coverage among the grown-ups to set the level of safety in the home. The circle can defend the baby no better than its weakest member allows.

Daily habits shield infants from flu alongside the vaccine. Keeping babies away from sick people and disinfecting surfaces both help. Covering coughs and sneezes with a tissue, wearing a mask, and washing hands the right way add more layers. The adults carry out all of those steps. A baby cannot wash its own hands, wear a mask, or step away from a coughing visitor. Even the protection that comes without a needle depends on the people nearby.

Each adult's decision about the vaccine in 2011 counted twice in a home with a young infant, once for the adult and once for the baby.

A flu shot could lower a person's chance of catching influenza by as much as 90%, according to the CDC position the gallery relayed in 2011. The agency paired that figure with a concession. The vaccine does not succeed every time. The two statements fit together. Studies put 90% at the ceiling of what the shot can achieve, and some recipients land below it. A person weighing the shot was buying a lower risk, with no guarantee attached.

Older adults and young children gain less from the vaccine, the CDC said in 2011. Age is one of several traits that shape the shot's results. Flu vaccine effectiveness can shift with a person's underlying medical conditions, past bouts of influenza, and earlier vaccinations. A healthy adult and an older relative with chronic illness can receive the same dose and come away with unequal defense.

Serious flu complications remain a target the shot can hit in cases where it fails to block infection, according to the 2011 guidance. An older adult or young child who comes down with the flu despite vaccination may avoid the worst turns of the illness. The benefit narrows in those groups without disappearing. A better match between the vaccine and the viruses spreading in a community raises the odds of strong protection against influenza illness and its complications.

Everyday conditions outside the controlled setting of a clinical trial can trim the payoff a vaccine provides. A figure drawn from a season of routine clinic visits can fall below the gallery's upper mark without contradicting it.

The myth grades the vaccine by the cases it fails to prevent. Judged that way, the shot looks weakest among older adults and young children, the same populations where the 2011 guidance credited it with heading off dangerous aftereffects. A tally of cases alone leaves out half the job the CDC assigned the shot for those patients.

Thimerosal, the ingredient behind the autism fear, showed no connection to the disorder across many studies, according to the CDC's position in 2011. The compound is a mercury-containing preservative, and some flu vaccines carried it that season. Critics had blamed it for a range of health problems and counted autism among them. The accusation pinned autism on a single component of a single shot, which handed researchers a narrow target to test.

Small doses of thimerosal caused no harm beyond redness and swelling at the injection site, the CDC's position held. Mercury on a vaccine ingredient list can alarm a parent, yet the amounts in a flu vaccine were small. The usual reactions stayed local, confined to the patch of skin around the needle. A few people may be allergic to thimerosal, though the CDC describes such reactions as rare. Critics described harm extending past the arm, and the evidence the agency relayed kept the common effects at the spot where the needle went in.

The no-link finding has outlasted the 2011 season. The CDC's vaccine-safety guidance concludes that research has turned up no connection between thimerosal in vaccines and autism. The agency points to numerous well-designed investigations that ruled out any role for the compound in causing the condition. Across the years between the 2011 gallery and the agency's current guidance, the answer on autism has held steady.

The fear hangs on one ingredient, and the findings left that ingredient with no case to answer. Thimerosal appeared in some 2011 flu vaccines and not in others. Versions without the preservative existed that season as well. A parent uneasy about mercury had a route to protection that left the compound out. A person who skipped vaccination over autism worries gave up immunity against influenza to avoid a danger the investigations never found. Of the two threats weighed in that choice, only influenza carried any evidence behind it.

Children between six months and eight years old who had never received a seasonal flu vaccine needed two doses at least four weeks apart in 2011, under the CDC's guidance at the time.

One vaccine covered the entire 2011 season, and most people needed a single dose of it. The gallery used those facts to puncture the idea that one shot leaves a person exposed. The myth takes a real rule and stretches it across the whole population, when the requirement applied to a narrow slice of young children.

The two-dose rule hinged on two conditions holding at once, the child's age and a blank vaccination record. An eight-year-old who had received a seasonal shot in a previous year fell outside it. So did a nine-year-old receiving a first flu shot. Age alone put that child past the cutoff.

The required spacing turns a young child's first flu season into a two-visit commitment, with the return trip landing a month or more after the first. Every week of delay on the opening shot pushes the follow-up back by the same week. A family that waits for a convenient moment to start the series also delays the day the child completes the course.

The CDC describes the first dose as a primer that readies the immune system, with the second dose delivering the actual protection. A child who needs both shots but receives one can end up with weaker protection or none, according to the CDC. Skipping the follow-up wastes much of what the first visit set in motion.

The CDC reports that children who have received two doses at any point need a single dose in the current season. The present-day test counts completed pairs of doses. The gallery's 2011 wording turned on whether a child had ever received a seasonal shot. Completing the pair in a child's first season sets up every one-shot season that follows.

Tamiflu and Relenza, two prescription-only antivirals, served as a backup against flu in the CDC position relayed in 2011, with the shot holding the front line. These drugs come as pills, liquids, and inhaled powders, and they treat the symptoms of an infection underway. Neither one can replace a vaccine that builds defenses before the virus arrives.

Speed decides whether these medicines help. In 2011, the guidance held that their benefit depended on a patient starting them within the first day or two of illness. Prompt treatment can ease the harshest flu effects and shorten the illness by an average of one day, according to the National Institute of Allergy and Infectious Diseases. The institute describes antiviral medicines as a way to help head off severe complications, including bacterial pneumonia. The early cutoff restricts their value to patients who seek care at the first signs of sickness.

A doctor's prescription stands between a patient and either drug. Pharmacies cannot sell flu antivirals without one. The medicines block influenza viruses from copying themselves inside the body. They act on influenza alone and offer nothing against other viruses that cause similar complaints. A patient with a flu-like illness who obtains a prescription may see no improvement from the drug if a different virus caused it. The visit needed to get that prescription can consume part of the brief period when the drugs work.

The CDC calls a yearly flu vaccine its first and most important step against flu and lists drugs to treat flu illness as another option. The agency's stance matches the order the 2011 gallery described, with antivirals waiting in reserve behind the shot. The vaccine aims to keep flu from taking hold. The medicines try to blunt a case in progress against a ticking clock. Anyone who skips the shot and counts on a prescription is betting on spotting the illness in time, reaching a doctor, and having influenza and not some other virus.

5 Best New Movies And Shows To Stream This Weekend On Netflix, Apple TV, Hulu, And More (Nov. 6-8)

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

It's the first full weekend of November, the weather is finally turning truly chilly, and Halloweekend is in the rear-view mirror. Whether you went all-out on your costume last weekend or you elected to slap a nametag with a different moniker on your shirt and call it a day (which is my preferred way to celebrate Halloween if asked to do so), this weekend is perfect for staying in and curling up with a fresh series or movie. So, what's new on the big streamers?

Between Apple, Peacock, Hulu, and Netflix, there's a decent spate of new releases heading to streaming this weekend (and some of them even dropped earlier in the week). Whether you want to watch a gripping spy drama, a truly ridiculous legal drama, a breezy sports comedy, a crime thriller tinged with romance, or a quick-paced series featuring one of the best and weirdest pairings in TV history, I've got you covered.

Director Adam Carter Rehmeier, known for indie films like "Snack Shack" and "Dinner in America," teamed up with modern scream queen Samara Weaving and horror staple Kyle Gallner for this year's "Carolina Caroline" — which just hit Netflix, marking its first home on a major streaming service. Weaving, who also led "Ready or Not 2: Here I Come" and "Over Your Dead Body" recently (and who's joining the Marvel Cinematic Universe to play Emma Frost), stars as Caroline Daniels, a gas station attendant who falls in love with Gallner's con artist Oliver Anderson. As the two make their way to South Carolina from rural Texas, they end up committing robbery after robbery, leaving destruction in their wake.

Weaving's Caroline and Gallner's Oliver make for a great modern Bonnie and Clyde, and "Carolina Caroline" is a gripping, entertaining, and extraordinarily fun film that also features Jon Gries ("Napoleon Dynamite," "The White Lotus") and Kyra Sedgwick in supporting roles. If you want a crime drama packed with plenty of romance, "Carolina Caroline" on Netflix is your best bet.

Congratulations to all bad TV enjoyers: One of the worst shows ever made is back for Season 2! Ryan Murphy's gleefully ludicrous legal drama "All's Fair," which undoubtedly gives real lawyers raging headaches if they dare to tune in, stars Kim Kardashian as high-powered lawyer Allura Grant, who works alongside Liberty Ronson (Naomi Watts), Emerald J. Greene-Ball (Niecy Nash-Betts), Dina Standish (Glenn Close), and Milan (Teyana Taylor) at a firm that handles divorces. Sarah Paulson, a regular Murphy collaborator, livens up proceedings as rival lawyer Carrington "Carr" Lane, who is unbelievably twisted and extremely fun, and Nash-Betts, Watts, and Close are all game to ham it up.

I don't think I'm blowing anyone's mind when I say Kim Kardashian is a generationally dreadful actor who should not even appear in commercials, let alone a scripted drama that could be dishy and fun if she wasn't swanning around with absolutely zero expression in every single scene. Still, if you dig wildly bad TV and just want to switch your brain off, check out "All's Fair." Besides, if nothing else, it has given us an incredible episode of its fellow Hulu series "The Kardashians" where Close, out of character, gets very drunk off martinis at Kris Jenner's house.

I love "30 Rock," I have immense respect for weird little guy and short king Daniel Radcliffe, and I've been riding for Bobby Moynihan since the first time I saw him perform as Drunk Uncle on "Saturday Night Live." Essentially, "The Fall and Rise of Reggie Dinkins" — which stars Tracy Morgan, was created by Robert Carlock and Sam Means, is executive produced by Tina Fey, and stars Morgan and Radcliffe as the unlikeliest and best duo in recent TV memory — was made for me in a lab. My enduring affection for this show aside, I think it's sure to delight pretty much anybody, and when Season 1 premiered in January, it immediately became one of the best shows of 2026.

The premise? Morgan plays Reggie Dinkins, a disgraced NFL star who was banned from the New York Jets and all other professional teams in the league after being caught betting on his games. (Hilariously, he only bet on his own performance to inspire himself to play better.) Thus, Reggie tries to regain his tattered reputation alongside his best friend and former teammate Rusty (Moynihan), his ex-wife and manager Monica (Erika Alexander), his young but extremely compatible fiancée Brina (Precious Joy), his son Carmelo (Jalyn Hall), and Radcliffe's Oscar-winning documentarian Arthur Tobin. With Arthur living in his palatial suburban New York mansion to make a documentary that will hopefully relaunch Reggie's career and get him into the NFL Hall of Fame, Reggie does his best to refurbish his image, and the results are extremely funny. "Reggie Dinkins" Season 2 dropped on NBC and Peacock on November 2, and if you're returning to this near-perfect series, welcome back. If you're new, go laugh your way through Season 1, then join us.

If you enjoyed Keira Knightley and Ben Whishaw's delightful spy drama "Black Doves" when it dropped back on Netflix in 2024, you'll be pleased to hear the series — created by Joe Barton — released its second season on the streamer on November 5. Across six episodes, we pick up where the show left off with Helen Dawson (Knightley), a secret Black Doves operative who sells secrets to the highest bidder and also happens to be married to a Conservative MP for a major UK constituency and Secretary of State for Defense, Wallace Webb (Andrew Buchan). In the beginning of Season 1, Helen is shocked and saddened to learn that her lover, Jason Davies (Andre Koji), has died under suspicious circumstances ... and her secret identity may now be at risk.

Throughout that first season, Helen teams up with her old friend and hired assassin Sam Young (Whishaw), who's sent by the Doves to help protect her. In Season 2, after a shocking cliffhanger ending, we follow Helen and Sam as he desperately tries to keep her safe, even as she's targeted by nefarious forces throughout England. Knightley and Whishaw are fantastic together, and if you sort of forgot "Black Doves" was a thing since Season 1 premiered 2 years ago, I'm happy to tell you that it's back and still an extremely fun and enthralling watch.

Apple TV has some of the best comedy series on television hiding on its service (which seems to get overlooked in favor of ones like Netflix, Hulu, and Prime Video), and if you're a fan of "Ted Lasso" but haven't watched "Stick," you're really missing out. Owen Wilson leads this sports comedy created by Jason Keller and stars as Pryce Cahill, a former golf pro nicknamed "The Stick" who's stuck selling clubs and coaching at a country club after years of a career decline. At work one day, Pryce meets a gifted young golfer, Santi Wheeler (Peter Dager), who quit golfing after some personal issues got in the way of his game. Determined to make Santi the next golf star, Pryce ends up roping him into a cross-country tour to show off his talents alongside Santi's mother Elena (Mariana Treviño), Pryce's former caddy Mitts (Marc Maron), and a onetime bartender and current caddy Zero Duffy (Lili Kay), all of whom pile into an RV to make the trip.

"Stick" Season 2 adds great actors like Timothy Olyphant (as Pryce's former golf rival Clark Ross) as regulars and guest stars like recent Emmy winner Rhea Seehorn as Pryce's former coach, with Billy Zane also joining Season 2 in an as-yet-unknown capacity. The first two episodes of the sophomore season dropped on November 4, so catch up before it continues its run.

Three Ways to Avoid CAPTCHA Prompts When Using a VPN

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

VPNs make it harder to track your online activity across websites, but it also makes them suspicious about your origins. When you borrow an IP address that hundreds of other people are also using for any number of reasons, you’re much more likely to get repeat verification requests from online firewalls.

That said, you don’t have to keep clicking on those traffic lights over and over. You can simply switch to a more reputable VPN server. Moreover, browsers now offer persistent access tokens to eliminate CAPTCHA requests, while VPNs offer dedicated IPs to keep sites from getting suspicious. Here are three easy fixes for repeat CAPTCHA prompts when using a VPN, so you don’t have to trade convenience for online privacy.

CAPTCHAs are simple puzzles that websites use to filter out bots and spam. They’re supposed to be easy for people but hard for robots, such as simple object recognition tasks or audio-visual challenges. However, they can still disrupt your browsing experience if they happen too frequently.

Most modern CAPTCHA providers, like Google's reCAPTCHA v3 or Cloudflare Turnstile, start working long before they give you a puzzle to solve. It usually starts with a risk score that studies your IP address, browser and device signals, or recent online activity.

If the CAPTCHA provider can’t confidently identify your request as human based on those signals, it requests that you complete a puzzle before deciding if the request should be blocked. That means when you get repeat CAPTCHA requests, there’s something affecting your browser’s risk score. For example, popular VPN servers spread the same IP address across any number of users, which can easily make a website mistake you for a bot farm. If you change your IP address frequently by switching servers often, that might look suspicious too.

Luckily, there are ways to deal with frequent CAPTCHA requests using tools and services available with your browser and VPN. Cloudflare has already cut its CAPTCHA requests by 91% using its Managed Challenge platform. Apple has also been shipping token-based verification on devices since iOS 16 and macOS Ventura. In June 2026, Cloudflare even announced a joint effort called the Private Access Control Tokens (PACT) standard with Chrome, Edge, Firefox, and Shopify.

While there’s no direct off switch for CAPTCHAs, you can use different fixes to improve your browser’s trust signals when using the internet. That way, you’re less likely to be hounded by verification requests.

The easiest fix for repeat CAPTCHAs is to stop connecting to whichever server your VPN app picks automatically. Proton recommends choosing servers with lower traffic load. In fact, its apps display load as a percentage to help you avoid the most crowded options. I'd also skip big traffic hubs like New York or London, since that's where most traffic originates from. Servers in smaller cities located nearby are often just as fast but never as populated.

You should also avoid changing IP addresses too frequently, which can look suspicious on its own. Pick a server that seems to behave well, then continue using it for at least one session. Also remember you’re more likely to get premium server options if you’re on a paid VPN plan. Major platforms like ExpressVPN, Surfshark, Proton, and Nord are particularly likely to have a larger server collection to pick from.

Costs nothing more than your current subscription

Hopping between servers often can backfire

Private Access Tokens (PAT) let you verify the authenticity of your request using your device rather than an IP address. Apple calls it Automatic Verification, where an Apple server validates your device and Apple Account to assign you an anonymous token.

To enable Automatic Verification on an iPhone or iPad, go to Settings > [your name] > Sign-In & Security > Automatic Verification. On a Mac, open System Settings > [your name] > Sign-In & Security > Automatic Verification.

Unfortunately, there’s no equivalent feature for Windows, Android, and Linux users. That said, Cloudflare offers the Silk extension for Chrome, Firefox, and Edge that serves a similar function. A quick look at the ratings will tell you it’s not as effective, but Cloudflare’s upcoming cross-browser standard called PACT seems promising once it is released.

Doesn’t share personal details with websites

Chrome and Firefox extensions can be unreliable

If you pay a bit extra for a dedicated IP add-on, VPN providers can assign you a unique IP address that isn’t shared with anyone else. For example, NordVPN offers dedicated IPs in 30 countries for $2-5 a month and Surfshark charges $3.75 a month for it.

There are also dedicated providers like Private Internet Access, which charges $3.99 per month for a zero-trust setup so not even their employees know which IP address is assigned to you. On the other hand, Proton VPN offers dedicated IPs only to business plan users, so you’ll need to be on the Workspace Standard, Premium, or Enterprise plans to get one.

That said, there is a trade-off here too. NordVPN itself admits that a dedicated IP makes it easier to link online activity back to a single user. Also, many websites can still tell that the IP address originated from a VPN data center rather than a consumer’s device.

Get unique IP addresses with pristine reputation

Works across desktop, mobile, and even smart TVs

Also functions as an allowlisted address for work

Add-ons cost more on top of the VPN subscription

Offers less anonymity than shared VPN servers

Kim Kardashian’s Beverly Hills Marital Villa Lists for $8.5 Million—Plus 4 More Luxury Homes for Sale

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

This week’s standout luxury listings include the Notting Hill blue-door townhouse, Diana Jenkins’s Hidden Hills mansion, and a Connecticut estate by a Frank Lloyd Wright protégé.

Even if you keep up with the Kardashians, you might not recognize this Beverly Hills house, which was one of Kim Kardashian’s first real estate purchases. The reality star bought the gated home 16 years ago for $3.4 million, and it served as the setting when basketball player Kris Humphries proposed to her in 2011. But the residence has gotten a major overhaul in the years since Kardashian sold it following the short-lived marriage, and now the Tuscan-style villa is on the market for nearly $8.5 million.

Other listings on our radar this week include another reality TV–linked estate: A former Real Housewife of Beverly Hills is parting with her 13,200-square-foot Hidden Hills house. On the East Coast, a striking Connecticut home by a Frank Lloyd Wright protégé is also on the market. And across the pond, the memorable blue-door townhouse from ‘90s rom-com Notting Hill in London is also for sale. Check them all out below.

One of Kim Kardashian’s earliest real estate purchases was this gated property in Beverly Hills, which she acquired in 2010 for $3.4 million. The Tuscan-style villa quickly became a familiar backdrop on her family’s E! reality show, Keeping Up with the Kardashians, and was even the site of Kris Humphries’s (televised) 2011 proposal to her. In the aftermath of their 72-day marriage, Kardashian sold the half-acre estate, and its new owners tapped fast-rising designer Sebastian Zuchowicki to oversee a complete overhaul. Now on the market for just under $8.5 million with Jack Harris and Michael Fahimian of Beverly Hills Estates, the five-bedroom home has a double-height entry foyer, custom kitchen cabinetry, and a primary suite with a fireplace, two walk-in closets, and a soaking tub.

Entrepreneur Diana Jenkins spent much of her time on The Real Housewives of Beverly Hills nesting in her sprawling Hidden Hills mansion with her newborn daughter, Eliyanah. Now, four years since leaving the Bravo reality series, the native Bosnian is returning to her home country, per Realtor.com—and listing the 13,200-square-foot mansion for $23 million with Sotheby’s. Set on 4.4 acres, the seven-bedroom house was built in 2019 and comes with a plethora of perks, among them an infinity-edge pool, barbecue, fitness studio, and home cinema.

It’s perhaps the most famous house in one of London’s most charming neighborhoods, so beloved that crowds are known to gather around it like the English version of Carrie Bradshaw’s West Village apartment. The blue-doored townhouse where Hugh Grant’s fictional bookseller lived and wooed Julia Roberts’s paparazzi-plagued Hollywood actress in 1999’s Notting Hill is now on the market. And the 2,750-square-foot house can be yours for roughly $10 million.

With its organic silhouette, cantilevered roofline, and clever use of space, Leuthold House by Allan Gelbin bears the hallmarks of his mentor, legendary architect Frank Lloyd Wright. Completed in 1966 on five rolling acres in New Canaan, the five-bedroom house spans roughly 5,700 square feet and underwent a restoration by Hobbs Inc. and JMK Architects in 2014. The interiors feature long expanses of glass and mahogany, while the grounds come with a spring-fed pond, a pool, a one-bedroom guest house, and 150 specimen trees. Listed for $6.2 million with John Engel and Melissa Engel of Douglas Elliman, the two-story house is on the market for the first time in 29 years.

One of the art world’s most famous couples once lived and worked in a gated townhouse a block from Washington Square Park. Jackson Pollock and Lee Krasner didn’t own the MacDougal Alley home—a brick-faced carriage house built in the 1870s—but rather stayed there in the winter of 1950 due to the generosity of its owner, a fellow celebrated artist and their friend Alfonso Ossorio. Now, Ossorio’s 1,500-square-foot residence is on the market for the first time since 1988, per the New York Post. The asking price for the two-bedroom, two-bath home is nearly $8 million.

Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

With the <i><em>Hunger Games</em></i> Press Tour, Have We Reached the Final Frontier of Method Dressing?

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The cast has offered a pointed take on the state of today’s red carpets

Another Hunger Games movie, a prequel to the initial series, premieres later this month, and with it comes another press tour full of fantastically far-fetched fashion. As the promotional events begin to stack up for Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping, we are reminded of the familiar lore of this dystopian empire: In a fictionalized future America called Panem, the 12 districts that supply resources to the rich and powerful people of the Capitol are forced to send two children to fight to the death each year. The excess of the Capitol is signaled in the films via outlandish style, which the newest film’s cast members have been attempting to channel in their recent looks. The goal is to be extreme—but in an era of extremes on the red carpet, these over-the-top ensembles actually don’t look so out of place.

Elle Fanning plays a young Effie Trinket, who is essentially a Capitol stylist dressing the young “tributes” in all sorts of fanciful fashions—and doing the same for herself. (In the first film series, the character wore a lot of McQueen.) So far, Fanning’s press looks have deliberately channeled that outlandishness. For a London fan screening, she wore a gown from Robert Wun’s Fall 2026 couture collection that subverted ideas of childlike wonder. Her ensemble began in miniature with a music-box-style ballerina atop a bubblegum pink fascinator. The corresponding human-size gown features a doubled-up bodice, the outer layer appearing to peel off, above a full tulle skirt, all in that same dusty pink.

For her other appearances, Fanning has established a de facto uniform of precisely tailored skirt suits, each paired with some sort of coordinating fascinator tilted just so above her brow. She has selected a soft pink pleated skirt suit from Valentino complete with a ruffled collar, gloves, and a butterfly-trimmed chapeau; a sharp-shouldered version in a deep, almost reptilian satin green from Demna’s Gucci; and a floral, bow-embellished one from Vivienne Westwood. The looks are decidedly feminine in a manner that is at once both futuristic and retro in very Capitol fashion.

Fanning’s stylist Samantha McMillen has been vocal about the references to the character, with captions like “Gucci Effie | @demna puts “The Hunger” into @thehungergames,” and “Elle bringing 🦋 “Sunrise” 🦋 to the Academy Gala.” And it’s not just Fanning doing this either. Costar Mckenna Grace recently recreated a Katniss Everdeen Capitol tour look in which Katniss’s dress quite literally caught fire. Created by Vietnamese designer Cong Tri, who is known for his sculptural silhouettes, the recreation nixed the pyrotechnics but printed graphic flames onto the skirt and bodice. With the help of stylist Molly Dickson, Grace also revived Katniss’s wedding dress with a frothy white, silver-embellished gown by Monique Lhuillier.

I haven’t seen the most recent films, but all of them examine the same concept of this dystopian society propped up by the Hunger Games themselves at different points on a timeline. In the first film specifically, the older Trinket uses their version of a press tour to craft a narrative around Katniss and her fellow tribute Peeta Mellark and win favor among their audience through larger-than-life fashion. The aforementioned fire dress referenced Everdeen’s district’s coal mining industry, and the wedding dress was meant to create the illusion of a love story between the two of them. The series of looks is designed to be projected to the surrounding communities—and garner as much attention as possible. The characters can quite literally benefit later in the life-and-death-style games from the loyalties and sympathies the theatrics spawn.

It’s only in recent years that this idea of method dressing, or theming press tour looks around an actor’s current project, has become commonplace. It began in earnest with the work of Law Roach and Zendaya around the Dune and Spider-Man franchises, Challengers, and The Drama. It was perhaps commercially crystallized in 2023 by Andrew Mukamal and Margot Robbie with Barbie—a press tour that spawned so much digital attention it earned its own coffee-table book—and, more recently, Wuthering Heights. Now, it has proof of concept, attracting stars like Cynthia Erivo and Ariana Grande in their promotion of Wicked, the new cast of Sense and Sensibility, and Lili Reinhart’s rom-com references for The Love Hypothesis. At its most reductive, the tactic is a surefire way to drum up conversation on social media; at its best, a clever way to add depth to a project’s storytelling.

As press tours become more and more oriented towards social media, it’s difficult to isolate the influence of method dressing on the red carpet’s current proclivity towards extremes. Everyone is trying to capitalize on the sort of hype that the Met Gala ensembles can drum up. Your reference needs to be loud enough to cut through the noise—pyrotechnics optional. It’s perhaps why avant-garde, couture, and couture-level designers like Wun, who dressed a large handful of stars at this year’s Met Gala, are carving out more and more space among these sorts of promotional events. At the same time, brands like Standing Ground and Matieres Fecales—it’s only a matter of time before Fanning wears one of those skirt suits—are also gaining traction as stylists treat the red carpet more like a magazine editorial than an occasion to get dressed up.

Then last night, Grace added another layer. She and Dickson have begun to look outside the films to reference looks of Jennifer Lawrence’s own. Lawrence played Katniss in the first movies. For a Madrid premiere, she wore a Miss Sohee couture dress that paid homage to the Givenchy naked dress Lawrence wore for this year’s Golden Globes. It all feels very meta, calling into question the role of fashion in these promotional spaces, real or fictional. On the IRL Hunger Games red carpet, the reference is dystopian extravagance—but with the rise of method dressing and the corresponding celebration of larger, louder looks, has dystopian extravagance already become the norm? In this case, it may not be life and death, but it could make or break your movie.

OpenAI reportedly raises an additional $11 billion at a $1.25 trillion valuation

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

OpenAI has reportedly raised $11 billion so far in a new funding round that values the AI lab at $1.25 trillion.

Bloomberg reported on the new funding on Friday, citing sources familiar with the matter. The $11 billion is part of a larger funding round that hasn’t closed yet, according to the outlet. The round is expected to close in the coming weeks and could total about $20 billion, a source told Bloomberg.

In late September, Bloomberg reported that OpenAI was looking to raise $30 billion in the upcoming round. The current figures fall well short of that target.

TechCrunch has reached out to OpenAI for comment and will update this story if it responds.

The news comes only hours after Bloomberg also reported that potential buyers of shares in the IPO of OpenAI’s chief competitor, Anthropic, are eyeing a substantially lower valuation than Anthropic had targeted. Bloomberg writes that some potential buyers believe Anthropic’s valuation could be as low as $1.1 trillion, compared with the $2 trillion that investors were said to be expecting roughly a month ago.

OpenAI previously intimated that it would go public this year but subsequently delayed its plans. There are expectations that the company will IPO next year at some point.

The market for AI hangs in a particularly precarious place. The Democratic landslide in the U.S. midterms — and overwhelmingly negative voter sentiment around data centers — has caused jitters throughout the market, with many anticipating potential regulatory challenges in the future.

UTA, FaZe vets launch ISO as a “new partner for talent”

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

A new talent firm is launching with a stacked roster of luminaries from the worlds of streaming, social media, and content creation. Mike Lee and Darren Yan are the leaders of ISO, which is kicking off with a roster of 90 top creators.

ISO (the name stands for “in search of”) is a wide-reaching management firm that touch categories like beauty, food, and art, but its roots are deepest in the gaming and streamer communities. Lee, who will serve as ISO’s CEO, was previously the Head of Gaming and Livestreaming at UTA Creators. Yan, ISO’s Head of Music, is the former Chief Talent Officer of FaZe Clan, where he worked to raise up some of the hottest talents on platforms like Twitch.

At ISO, Lee and Yan have assembled a deep roster that includes big names like PlaqueBoyMax, Jasontheween, Ironmouse, Lacy, Vanillamace, Kyedae, jessechrisss, Angelo Marasigan, and RDCWorld. Some of those creators, like PlaqueBoyMax and Lacy, were previously members of FaZe Clan. Others, like Ironmouse and Vanillamace, have history with UTA.

Daily industry updates, straight to your inbox.

Influence Media Partners is backing ISO as the new firm takes shape. “The next household name is already online, building an audience, and ISO was built for that talent. Today’s gatekeepers are algorithms, so understanding discovery, engagement and how content travels is essential,” Lee said in a statement. “Every creator we work with is running a real business, and our job is to give it room to grow through original IP, products, live events, and expansion into China and platforms like TikTok Shop, where most of the industry still isn’t set up to compete.”

Lee is serious about ISO’s inroads into East Asia. A press release highlighted internation talent like Vietnam’s Son-Tung MTP and China’s Xin Liu, both of whom are part of the ISO equation.

ISO also plans to innovate on the brand-facing side of its business. Its core team has experience at companies like the NFL, Red Bull, and Fanatics, which are on board as talent partners. Matt Alsberg, ISO’s Head of Talent, previously held roles at PlayStation and Twitch.

With a wealth of ideas and an abundance of talent up and down its roster, ISO is ready to become a management company with deep knowledge of the creator ecosystem. The creators working with ISO are known for their brash, bold-faced personalities — and that attitude seems to fit the firm to a tee.

Godzilla Minus Zero's King Ghidorah Has One Scene That Was 55 Years In The Making

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

This article contains minor spoilers for "Godzilla Minus Zero."

"Godzilla Minus Zero" is here, with director Takashi Yamazaki once again getting to put his stamp on the King of the Monsters. He also got to offer up his take on another classic kaiju this time around in the form of King Ghidorah. The monster's appearance was a poorly kept secret, with Ghidorah's design in "Godzilla Minus Zero" leaking thanks to merchandise. But in any event, the monster's inclusion helped resolve a 55-year-old dream for the filmmaker.

According to Reuters, Yamazaki grew up in the Japanese ‌city of Matsumoto. A big "Godzilla" fan, he was particularly excited about 1964's "Ghidorah, the Three-Headed Monster," one of the best "Godzilla" movies of all time. In particular, he hoped to see the kaiju wreck shop in his hometown. Unfortunately, Matsumoto only appeared fleetingly in the movie after Ghidorah's emergence.

Given that "Godzilla Minus One" became the highest-grossing Japanese Godzilla movie ever en route to winning an Oscar, Yamazaki was given a lot of creative freedom on the sequel. He used that freedom to show Ghidorah doing what he wanted to see as a child. "Minus Zero" prominently features Matsumoto, with Ghidorah carving a path of destruction there relatively early on in the movie's runtime. Here's what the filmmaker had to say about it:

"55 years later, I had King ‌Ghidorah ​appear in my own film. I had the chance ⁠to make the movie I ⁠wanted to watch back then, and I created the footage that I wanted to show my younger self, as if to say, 'This is what you wanted to see, isn't it?'"

"So, my dream has finally come true after ​55 years, and I am truly happy," Takashi Yamazaki concluded.

The sequel picks up two years after the events of "Minus One" as Japan is still trying to put itself back together in the aftermath of Godzilla's attacks. Things are once again thrown into chaos when a new, mysterious, dragon-like monster emerges. It's not long before Godzilla is back as well, leaving the people of Japan scrambling to figure out how to deal with not one but two kaiju.

"Godzilla Minus Zero" is rated R, which meant that Yamazaki didn't have to hold back at all. When it came to having King Ghidorah destroy his hometown, he certainly made the most of that R rating. In a bizarre method of showing pride for the place he came from, he made the most of Matsumoto in his latest cinematic effort. Being from Japan and having your home shown off in a "Godzilla" movie is, understandably, something to be proud of.

King Ghidorah was the movie's "surprise" villain who has a long history with the franchise. "Ghidorah, the Three-Headed Monster" was his first of many, many appearances across the many eras of this franchise. In recent years, he's been showcased in everything from "Godzilla: King of the Monsters" in the MonsterVerse to the animated "Godzilla: The Planet Eater" on Netflix. He's without a doubt Godzilla's most notorious foe.

"Godzilla Minus Zero" is in theaters now.

Milania Giudice 'Did Not Leave' BFF Victoria Zardoya's Side in the Hospital

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Milania Giudice stayed by her best friend Victoria Zardoya’s side in the days before her death.

“I just know Milania has been through a lot,” Real Housewives of New Jersey star Jennifer Aydin told The Daily Mail in an interview published on Friday, November 6. “She lost her best friend. Her best friend was an organ donor. When you are an organ donor, you have to wait for them to come and retrieve your organs.”

She continued, “So you will be in the bed for sometimes days, maybe even over a week. And Milania did not leave her best friend’s side … She was in the hospital with her until they retrieved her organs. She did not leave her side, and Teresa flew to Tampa to support Milania and Victoria’s family. And that’s a lot for anybody to handle, let alone, you know, a young adult. So, she’s had a lot thrown her way.”

Aydin, 49 — a close friend of RHONJ costar and Milania’s mom, Teresa Giudice — said that Milania, 20, was at the hospital with Zardoya for “at least five days or more.”

Zardoya, Milania’s childhood best friend, died at age 20 in July after suffering an accidental fall while exploring the ruins of Fort Dade from the Spanish-American War during a trip to Egmont Key, an island off the coast of Tampa Bay, Florida.

According to the autopsy report obtained by Us on Tuesday, November 3, Zardoya’s cause of death was listed as “craniocerebral trauma,” including skull fractures. The manner of death is listed as “accident (fall from height and injured self).”

According to the National Institute of Neurological Disorders and Stroke, craniocerebral trauma, or traumatic brain injury (TBI), is damage to the brain caused by an external force, commonly categorized into penetrating and non-penetrating injuries. It results from events like falls, crashes or blows to the head.

Toxicology test results determined that Zardoya was positive for ketamine and norketamine, a substance produced when the body breaks down the drug. The results also showed alcohol and etomidate, a medication used for sedation.

A postmortem examination from the Sarasota, Florida, medical examiner’s office confirmed Zardoya was an organ donor and donated both of her lungs, heart, liver, both kidneys and more following her death.

Milania has been open about her struggles with grief following Victoria’s death.

“If this summer taught me anything, it is to never judge anybody no matter what you see, what you hear. Never judge anybody. It also taught me that life is all about sacrifice,” she said in an Instagram video last month after she made headlines for a tense exchange with her mom at the Tampa International Airport.

Milania continued, “Empathy is a trait that I’ve personally have always had. When I love, I want to move mountains for you. When you’re upset, I’m upset. When you’re angry, I’m angry. I’m a ride-or-die friend. I’m ride or die for my family.”

Milania entered a treatment center last month after she was diagnosed with bipolar disorder, depression, anxiety, PTSD and ADHD.

“I am not ashamed of any of my mental health disorders because I’m currently in a treatment center,” she said in a video from the center. “My mom’s actually on her way to come visit me today, and I do work on myself. But you know what? I am only 20 years old, and this summer hit me like a bus.”

Teresa, 54, has also shown her support for Milania in the wake of Zardoya’s death.

“As a mother, there is nothing more important to me than the health, safety, and well-being of my children,” she said in a statement in September. “The past few months have been incredibly difficult for our family, and I feel it is important to say something while also protecting my daughter during an extremely vulnerable time in her life.”

Teresa added, “Grief affects everyone differently, and watching your child try to process that kind of loss is something no parent can ever truly prepare for. Right now, my only focus is my daughter — making sure she is surrounded by love, family, support, and the care she needs.”
